A flamboyant figure and a known practitioner of alchemy, he was a founding member of the Royal Society. --- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction of the Italian poet and alchemist Isabella Cortese, whose verses the author translates to illustrate the foundational principles of the alchemical "Art."</meta>

Salt removes the unpleasant stench <note>original: "fetor ingrato"</note>
And makes every part white,
It dissolves and liquefies well,
It also purges everything;
And being firm and upright,
It holds the fugitives <term>fugitives: volatile substances, usually Mercury, that tend to evaporate or "flee" when heated</term> tight,
And without salt,
Our practice is worth nothing.

## OTHER VERSES

The art lies in pure waters
And cares to do nothing else;
It generates the tincture <term>tincture: the transformative essence or elixir that changes base metals into gold</term>,
A thing that endures in the fire;
Mercury is accustomed to melting
Every leaf of the sun <note>original: "foliato sole," a poetic reference to gold leaf or gold in its metallic form</note>;
It dissolves it and makes it soft,
It takes the soul from the body,
And afterwards congeals it,
For the one to whom God reveals it.

# QUATRAIN

->*This peerless Phoenix with its golden*<-
->*tresses,*<- <note>original: "tresse blonde." In alchemical poetry, golden hair often signifies the arrival of the "Golden" or solar state of the matter.</note>
->*Which Phoebus sends us from the lineage*<-
->*of the Gods,*<- <note>Phoebus is the Greco-Roman god of the Sun (Apollo); in alchemy, he represents the solar principle, the source of heat, and the metal Gold.</note>
->*Encompassing three-in-one, descending*<-
->*from high places,*<- <note>original: "tripl'en un." This refers to the alchemical trinity of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which must be unified into a single substance to achieve the Great Work.</note>
->*To see it here below, victorious over*<-
->*the world.*<-

### VOCABULARY

| Original Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Phœnix** | **Phoenix** | A mythical bird that rises from its own ashes, symbolizing the final stage of the alchemical process (the *Rubedo* or Red Work). |
| **Phœbus** | **Phoebus** | A name for Apollo, the sun god, representing the "Solar" or gold-making power. |
| **Quatrain** | **Quatrain** | A poem or stanza consisting of four lines. |
| **Dieux** | **Gods** | References the "race of the Gods" to suggest the divine or celestial origin of alchemical knowledge. |
| **Victorieux** | **Victorious** | Signifies the successful transmutation and the Stone’s power to overcome the "imperfections" of base metals. |
| **Nompareil** | **Peerless** | Meaning unequaled or without match; referring to the unique perfection of the Philosopher’s Stone. |

<summary>This page features a four-line poem (quatrain) using the celestial and mythical imagery of the Phoenix and Phoebus to celebrate the successful creation of the alchemical elixir. It emphasizes the unification of the "three-in-one" principles and the divine origin of the art.</summary>

<keywords>Phoenix, Phoebus, Apollo, Sun, Trinity, Three-in-one, Rubedo, Red Work, Victory, Alchemy, Poetry</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical theme of the Phoenix introduced on the previous page. While the previous quatrain celebrated the completion of the Great Work, these stanzas explain the philosophical and elemental foundations—specifically the "Tria Prima"—that lead to the creation of the Phoenix (the Philosopher's Stone).</meta>

# STANZAS ON THE FOLLOWING FIGURE
## of the Phoenix.

->I.<-

*God, who composed all from the purest of the earth,*
*When this Chaos* <note>In alchemy, Chaos refers to the "Prima Materia" or First Matter—the formless, raw state of substance before it is purified.</note> *was made, and all that it encloses,*
*He placed it under the power of all Nature,*
*Who causes us to see, in the light of day, Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.* <note>These three substances, known as the "Tria Prima," were identified by Paracelsus as the fundamental components of all matter: Salt (body/solidity), Sulfur (soul/inflammability), and Mercury (spirit/fluidity).</note>

->II.<-

*This domain* <note>original: "pourpris." This archaic term refers to an enclosure or a sovereign territory.</note> *being made, and this round mass,*
*The Elements united and all that is in the world,*
*The seeds that are within, all that exists in Nature,*
*They are born through Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.*

->III.<-

*Through them, all power is made manifest,*
*The resulting being created with the same wisdom.*
*As long as Nature continues from day to day,*
*She makes Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury act beneath the Heavens.*

->IIII.<-

*The more everything continues with great firmness,*
*The more everything that is made is solidly established,*
*And Nature disposes herself through individuals,* <note>original: "Indiuidus." In this context, it refers to specific, distinct living things that embody the universal principles.</note>
*By which Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury are remade.*

->V.<-

*Then, furthermore, she always multiplies herself,*
*Causing the earth, through alteration, to be filled*
*With moisture, so that every year in rich Nature,*
*The spirit recomposes itself into Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.*

### VOCABULARY

| Original Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Phœnix** | **Phoenix** | The ultimate symbol of alchemical rebirth and the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone. |
| **Nature** | **Nature** | Viewed here as a divine agent or "alchemist" that manages the transformation of elements. |
| **Sel** | **Salt** | The principle of fixity, crystallization, and the physical body. |
| **Soulfre** | **Sulfur** | The principle of combustibility, heat, and the "soul" of the matter. |
| **Mercure** | **Mercury** | The principle of fusibility, volatility, and the "spirit" that connects body and soul. |
| **Chaos** | **Chaos** | The initial, disordered state of matter before the alchemist (or God) brings order to it. |
| **Elemens** | **Elements** | The traditional four elements (Earth, Air, Fire, Water) which the Tria Prima reside within. |
| **Alchimie** | **Alchemy** | The art and science of perfecting nature and transmuting base substances into noble ones. |

<summary>This page consists of five stanzas explaining that all physical existence and natural growth are governed by the three alchemical principles: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. It frames God as the original creator of the "Chaos" and Nature as the ongoing force that cycles these three principles to sustain life and matter.</summary>
<keywords>Tria Prima, Salt, Sulfur, Mercury, Chaos, Nature, Alchemical elements, Prime Matter, Generation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page provides the visual accompaniment to the five stanzas translated on the previous page. It illustrates the "Following Figure of the Phoenix," visually encoding the relationships between the Tria Prima (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) and the celestial forces that govern them.</meta>

# EMBLEMATIC FIGURE
## of the Phoenix and the Alchemical Principles.

-> [An engraving depicting the spiritual and material components of the Great Work] <-

At the highest point of the composition stands a crowned, bearded, and naked man <note>This figure represents Saturn (or Chronos), the god of time and the ruler of lead. In the alchemical process, Saturn signifies the initial stage of "blackening" (*nigredo*), where the raw matter is broken down through time and patience.</note>. In his hands, he carries a scythe—a symbol of the harvest and the "death" of the old form—and a pair of dividers, representing the divine geometry used to measure the heavens and the earth. To his left, seven drops fall like rain <note>These represent the seven planetary metals or the "celestial dew" required for the purification of the matter.</note>, while to his right, a fierce mass of flames rises.

Within the central triangle of the figure, a **Phoenix** is seen rising from its own ashes amidst the fire. 

<note>The Phoenix is the ultimate symbol of the Philosopher’s Stone; just as the bird is reborn from its own destruction, the alchemist’s materials are purified through fire to reach a state of perfection.</note>

Affixed to the base of this triangle is the symbol of the **Cross**, signifying the union of the four elements.

In the middle section of the engraving, three celestial signs are arranged:
*   The crescent **Moon** (Luna) with a human face on the left.
*   The radiant **Sun** (Sol) with a human face on the right.
*   The symbol for **Mercury** <note>In alchemy, Mercury is the "Mediator," the spirit that bridges the gap between the Sun (Soul/Sulfur) and the Moon (Body/Salt).</note> positioned prominently in the center.

At the very bottom, representing the terrestrial realm, sits a **solid cube** upon a mound of earth.

<note>The cube, or "cubic stone," represents the element of Earth and the principle of Salt. It is the fixed, stable foundation of the material world and the "cornerstone" of the philosophical work.</note>

The ground around this cube is lush with growth, featuring **lilies** on the left and **roses** on the right <note>The white lily and red rose frequently symbolize the "White" and "Red" stages of the alchemical process—the transition from purity to the final, perfected Stone.</note>, all set against a sky of dense, rhythmic lines.

<summary>This page features a complex alchemical engraving that visually summarizes the "Great Work," depicting the Phoenix rising from a triangle supported by the Tria Prima (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) and governed by Saturn.</summary>

-># PREFACE.<-

Furthermore, since our **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical "Great Work."</note> does not derive its essence or its birth from combustible things (seeing as it even fights against fire and withstands, without being in any way harmed, all its efforts and traps), do not seek it in such materials, in which almighty nature cannot place it.

For example, if someone were to say that it is of a **vegetable** nature—which is nonetheless impossible, although there appears in it a certain vegetable quality <note>In alchemy, "vegetable" often refers to the power of growth or a specific stage of the process, rather than a literal plant.</note>—you must know that if our **Lunary** <note>original: "lunaire". **Metals**, I want you to know it thus: First, the **celestial influence** <note>celestial influence: The spiritual or astrological power believed to flow from the heavens into the physical world to direct the growth of matter.</note> descends from above by the will and commandment of God, and mixes with the virtues and properties of the **Stars**. A circular alchemical woodcut emblem. Around the circular border is the Latin phrase "Visit the Interior of the Earth; by Rectifying, You Will Find the Hidden Stone" <note>original: *Visita Interiora Terræ Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem*. The first letters of each word in this Latin phrase form the acronym **VITRIOL**. In alchemy, this refers both to a physical chemical agent and a spiritual command to look within oneself to find the "Stone" or true essence.</note>

Inside the circle:
- At the top center is a chalice or goblet <note>representing the alchemical vessel or the "Cup of Hermes."</note>
- To the left of the chalice are symbols for the Sun (representing Gold) and Mars (representing Iron).
- To the right are symbols for the Moon (representing Silver) and Venus (representing Copper).
- Below the chalice is the symbol for Saturn (representing Lead) inside a dark orb, and to its right, the symbol for Jupiter (representing Tin). The chalice stem is formed by the symbol for Mercury (representing Quicksilver).
- Two hands emerge from clouds on the left and right sides of the circle, pointing inward toward the center.
- At the bottom, three shields are linked by a central ring. The left shield depicts a lion rampant <note>the "Red Lion," often symbolizing the fixed principle or sulfur.</note>. The right shield depicts a winged creature, likely a phoenix or eagle <note>symbolizing the volatile principle or the stage of sublimation and rebirth.</note>. The bottom shield contains an eight-pointed star.
- Next to the lion shield is a textured orb (possibly the moon). Next to the phoenix shield is a globe marked with grid lines.
- In the very center, between the shields, is an orb topped with a cross <note>term: *globus cruciger*; a symbol of terrestrial and spiritual dominion, representing the completed "Philosopher's Stone" which unites the physical and divine.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the introductory narrative as the master departs and the students begin their alchemical labors. It is prominently illustrated with the VITRIOL emblem, a classic symbolic map of the materials and spiritual goals of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>VITRIOL, Philosopher's Stone, divine wisdom, planetary symbols, alchemy, woodcut, Great Work, transmutation</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the series of the "Twelve Keys," a sequence of symbolic operations leading to the creation of the Philosopher's Stone. It follows the introductory woodcut which provided the VITRIOL acronym as a guide for the seeker to "look within."</meta>

# KEY I.

<insert>the Lame One (Vulcan)</insert> <note>original: *le Boiteux*. Vulcan, the Roman god of fire and the forge, is famously depicted with a limp. In alchemical practice, "Vulcan" refers to the secret, controlled heat of the furnace required to "cook" and transform the base materials.</note>

[An engraving by Jean Gobille illustrating the first step of the alchemical process.]

In the center of this composition, a **King** and **Queen** stand in ornate robes, representing the two primary principles of alchemy: the Solar/Sulfur (the King) and the Lunar/Mercury (the Queen). Their union is the goal of the work. The King holds a scepter and a plant with three flowers, representing the "triple" nature of the work or the three principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.

To the left, a gaunt, semi-clothed man—the god **Saturn**—sits holding a scythe, the symbol of time and harvest. He tends a small fire with a vessel upon it. In alchemy, Saturn represents the metal **Lead** and the initial stage of the work known as the *Nigredo* or "blackening," where the material is broken down and "dies" before it can be reborn.

To the right, a **wolf** leaps over a flaming brazier that holds a crucible. This "Grey Wolf" is a classic alchemical code for the mineral **antimony**. Just as antimony was used in metallurgy to purify gold by consuming all other metals, the "wolf" here represents the purification of the King (the Gold of the wise) before it can enter the next stage of transformation.

In the background, a sprawling castle or fortified city sits upon a hill, representing the "Citadel of Truth" or the completed Great Work.

At the bottom center, the artist has signed: "J. Gobille made this." <note>original: *J. Gobille fecit.* Jean Gobille was a 17th-century engraver known for his work on alchemical treatises.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the First Key of alchemical transformation, depicting the purification of the royal principles (Gold and Silver) through the "Grey Wolf" of antimony and the influence of Saturn (Lead/Time).</summary>
<keywords>Key I, alchemy, King and Queen, Saturn, Grey Wolf, antimony, Vulcan, Jean Gobille, purification, Nigredo</keywords>

# BOOK TWO
## CONTAINING THE FIRST KEY OF the Work of the Philosophers.
### CHAPTER I.
#### *On the preparation of the first matter.*

Know, my friend, that all unclean and leprous bodies <note>In alchemical texts, "leprous" is a technical term used to describe base metals that are full of impurities or "corruption" that must be cleansed before they can be transformed.</note> are not suitable for our Work <note>The "Work" (often capitalized as the Great Work or *Magnum Opus*) refers to the multi-stage process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, for their leprosy and impurity not only can produce nothing good, but also hinder that which is suitable from being able to produce.

->Every merchant’s merchandise<- <note>This centered phrase suggests that the materials of alchemy are like goods in a market; one must be discerning to choose the high-quality "merchandise" and reject the "counterfeit" or impure.</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the second book, introducing the First Key of alchemy which focuses on selecting the correct "first matter" by rejecting impure or "leprous" substances.</summary>

taken from the mines is sold each at its own price; but when it is adulterated <note>French: *falſifiée*. In the context of early chemistry and mining, this refers to minerals that have been "stretched" with cheaper materials or are naturally of poor quality.</note>, it is rendered useless, because it is spoiled; and not being similar to the natural form, it cannot perform the required operations.

Just as the Physician purges the inside of the body and cleanses away all filth through medicines, in the very same way, our "bodies" <note>In alchemy, metals were often called "bodies" (corpora). The alchemist treats these minerals as if they were living organisms that can be sick (impure) or healthy (pure).</note> must be purged and cleansed of all their impurities. to drive away diseases, because it penetrates, but with destruction and corruption of the subject, and engenders many more diseases; and although one may drive out one peg by another <note>original: "poufser vne cheuille par vne cheuille." to drive away diseases, because it penetrates, but with destruction and corruption of the subject, and engenders many more diseases; and although one may drive out one peg by another <note>original: "poufser vne cheuille par vne cheuille." This is an idiom for using one strong force to displace another, similar to the medical concept of "like cures like."</note>, in the same way we must drive out poison with poison; it is nevertheless necessary that our fountain be totally purged of it, and rendered entirely free of corrosion <term>corrosion: the caustic, burning quality of the "strong waters" or acids used to dissolve metals; if not removed, the "medicine" would destroy the patient's body.</term>.

One cuts down every tree that does not bring forth good and fragrant fruit, and grafts onto the trunk a better graft; this being done, the trunk produces a branch, and from there a fruitful tree is made, according to the gardener's desire.

The Sovereign <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Sovereign or King represents Gold or the Sun, the ultimate goal of the Great Work.</note> travels through six celestial cities <note>These represent the six other metals known to alchemy (Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, and Lead) and their corresponding planets.</note>; he makes his residence in the seventh, because his Royal palace there is adorned and embellished with gold, and with gilded buildings.

HEADER: BOOK II.

If you understand what I have just said, you have opened the first door of the first Key, and have passed the first barrier; but if you still see nothing <note>The original French idiom "n'y voy encores goutte" literally means "do not see a drop," meaning to be completely in the dark or confused.</note>, and see no light, you may handle and look at the glass <note>Referring to the alchemical flask or "philosopher's egg" where the transformation occurs.</note> as much as you like, it will serve you nothing, and your physical sight will not help you in any way to find at the end what you lack at the beginning, for I will say no more of this Key, as Luce Papirius <note>Likely a reference to Lucius Papirius, a name appearing in Roman history, here cited as a traditional authority or source of hermetic wisdom.</note> has taught me.

## BOOK II.

...ebb and flow <term>flux and reflux; the rising and falling of the tides</term> of the same, caused by the sympathy and influence of the celestial bodies; for at each ebb it brings them no small amount of goods, but a great quantity of precious riches.

A bride-to-be is dressed in beautiful and rich garments so that her husband finds her beautiful, and seeing her thus adorned, becomes enamored with her; but when they are to sleep together <note>The "nakedness" of the bride is a recurring motif in alchemy, representing the stripping away of impurities or "superfluities" to reveal the pure, primary substance (Materia Prima).</note>, all her various clothes are taken from her, and not a single one is left except that which she brought with her at birth, from her mother’s womb.

In just the same way, when we must marry our bridegroom Apollo <note>Apollo: the alchemical symbol for Gold or the Sun.</note> to his Diana <note>Diana: the alchemical symbol for Silver or the Moon.</note>; we must make various kinds of clothing for them, diligently wash their heads, and even their whole bodies, with water that must be prepared with much distil- <note>Original: "distil-", likely completing as "distillation" on the following page.</note>

# THE FOURTH KEY
## of the Work of the Philosophers. <note>original: "l’œuvre des Philosophes." This refers to the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the process of creating the Philosophers' Stone.</note>
### CHAPTER IV.

All flesh born of the earth shall be dissolved, and shall return to the earth, so that this earthly thing alone, aided by the influence of the Heavens, may bring forth a **new germ** <note>original: "germe." In alchemy, this is the "seed" or potential within matter that allows for transformation and growth.</note>; for if no earth is formed, no **resurrection** <note>The alchemist views the chemical process as a cycle of death (dissolution) and resurrection (re-solidification into a more perfect form).</note> can take place in our work, because the <term>balm of nature</term> <note>A vital, life-preserving essence believed by alchemists to exist within all matter, preventing total decay and allowing for rebirth.</note> is hidden in the earth, as is also the **Salt** <note>original: "Sel." In the Tria Prima (Three Primes) of alchemy, Salt represents the principle of fixity, the body, and the physical remains after combustion.</note> of those who have sought there the knowledge of all things.

On the day of judgment, the world shall be judged by fire, and that which was

...made from nothing, will be reduced to ash by fire; from this ash will be reborn a **Phoenix**, for within it is hidden the true <term>tartar</term><note>original: "tartre"; in alchemical practice, this refers to the crystalline sediment or essential salt used as a powerful solvent.</note>, which, once dissolved, allows one to open the strongest iron locks of the **Royal palace**. <note>The "Royal Palace" is a metaphorical term for the inner sanctum of nature or the final stage of the alchemical "Great Work."</note>

After the **general conflagration**, there will be a new earth, & new Heavens, & a new man, far more splendid & glorious than he was when he lived in the first world, because he will be **clarified**. <note>"Clarified" (clarifié) here suggests being made luminous, pure, and transparent, similar to the concept of a "glorified body."</note>

->Engraved by J. Gobille<- <note>original: "I. Gobille fecit"; Latin for "J. Gobille made this."</note>

<note>The **Fifth Key** of Basil Valentine is traditionally interpreted as the stage where the alchemist must regulate the "vital heat" or "secret fire." The man with the bellows represents the active application of breath and heat to the work. The woman holding the five-flowered plant likely symbolizes the **Quintessence** (the "fifth essence"), the purified spirit extracted from the four elements. The **Crowned Lion** represents the fixed metallic principle—often associated with Gold—which is being "wounded" by **Cupid's arrow**, signifying the desire for chemical union and the softening of the "hard" metal so it may be transformed.</note>

# SIXTH KEY of the Work of the Philosophers.

## CHAPTER VI.

The male without the female is but a half-body, just as the female is without the male; for being one without the other, they cannot engender and multiply their species.    Every Element is subject to generation and corruption; this is why every lover of wisdom must know that in each of these, the other three are occultly <note>hidden or secret</note> contained. # BOOK II.

Although each <term>Element</term> <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note> is formally distinguished from each of the others, this does not mean that they are therefore separated from the whole, as is clearly seen in the separation of the Elements by <term>distillation</term> <note>A chemical process of heating and cooling to purify a substance</note>.

Now, so that the ignorant person does not consider my discourse frivolous and serving no purpose, I wish to demonstrate it to you by sufficient proofs: Learn then, you who are curious to know the dissection and <term>anatomy of nature</term> and the separation of the Elements, that in the distillation of the earth, the **Air**, being lighter than the other two, distills first, then afterwards the **Water**; the **Fire**, because of its spiritual nature common to both and its natural sympathy, is joined with the Air; the **Earth** remains at the bottom and contains the <term>Salt of Glory</term> <note>original: "Sel de gloire"; likely referring to the "fixed salt" or the purified essence remaining after combustion, often associated with the "Philosopher's Stone" in alchemy</note>. In the distilla—

By the distillation of water, fire and air emerge first, then the water follows, while the earthy part always remains at the bottom. In the same way, from fire reduced into a visible and more material substance than is usual, one can extract fire, air, water, and earth, and preserve them separately. Similarly, air exists within the three others, as not one of them can function without it. The earth is nothing and can produce nothing without air; fire cannot burn or live without it; and water, lacking air, causes no generation <note>In alchemy, "generation" refers to the creation or growth of new matter or life</note>. Furthermore, air consumes nothing and dries no moisture without natural heat. Finding, therefore, a heat within the air, it follows that there must be fire within it, for everything that is of a hot and dry nature must also participate in the nature of fire. This is why all four elements <note>The classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth</note>—

# BOOK II.

And this **putrefaction** <note>In alchemy, putrefaction refers to the breakdown or "death" of a substance, which was believed to be a necessary first step for its rebirth or transformation into a higher state.</note> must be carried out through the operations of the elements; not because the elements are—as I have already taught—their seed, but because the **metallic seed**, taking its birth from a celestial, astral, and elementary being, and having been reduced into a perceptible body, must be putrefied by means of the elements.

Furthermore, note that **wine** has a **volatile spirit**, for in distilling it, the spirit comes out first and the **phlegm** <note>phlegm: a technical term for the watery, tasteless, and non-combustible part of a distilled liquid.</note> last. However, when it is turned into **vinegar** by continuous heat, its spirit is no longer so volatile; for in the distillation of vinegar, the watery phlegm rises first to the top of the **alembic** <note>alembic: a classic distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for purifying liquids.</note>, and the spirit last. And although it is the same matter in both cases, there are nevertheless very different qualities in vinegar than in wine, because the vinegar is no longer

<summary>The author explains that metallic seeds must undergo elemental putrefaction and uses the distillation of wine versus vinegar to illustrate how chemical properties and volatility change through heat.</summary>

# THE TWELVE KEYS

wine, but rather a putrefaction of the wine, which by continuous heat has been changed into vinegar. Everything that is extracted by wine or by its spirit <note>original: "esprit"; refers to distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>, and rectified in a circulatory vessel <note>A "pelican" or similar sealed glass vessel designed for continuous distillation and condensation</note>, has far different strengths and operations than that which is extracted by vinegar. For if one extracts the glass of <term>Antimony</term> <note>A metallic element used in alchemy; "glass of antimony" is a translucent vitreous form produced by roasting and melting the ore</note> by wine or by its spirit, it is too laxative and purges with too much vehemence from above <note>meaning it induces violent vomiting</note>, insofar as its poisonous virtue has not been overcome and extinguished; it still remains within the boundaries of poison. 

But if one extracts it by distilled vinegar, that which comes from it will be of a beautiful color. Then, if by removing the vinegar using a <term>Bain-marie</term> <note>A water bath used for gentle, even heating</note>, one washes the yellow powder that remains at the bottom—pouring common water over it many times, and as many times drawing it off until all the strength of the vinegar is removed—there is created a sweet powder.

# NINTH KEY
## of the work of the Philosophers.

### CHAPTER IX.

SATURN, the highest of the Planets, is the lowest & most abject in our <term>magistery</term> <note>The "magistery" refers to the mastery of the alchemical process or the Great Work itself</note>; he nevertheless holds the principal Key, and being vile, and having almost no authority, he holds the most beautiful place; and although by his will he has acquired the highest position above the highest Planets, he must nevertheless fall to the lowest, by having his wings cut, and his obscure light being greatly diminished, and through his death comes all the perfection of the work.

### KEY XI.

<note>This illustration acts as the visual centerpiece for the Eleventh Key. It depicts an alchemical allegory focused on the "multiplication" of the Stone—the process of increasing its power and quantity.</note>

->[Illustration]<-

In the foreground of a vast, mountainous landscape, two powerful **lions** are locked in a fierce struggle, each biting the other's mouth. This represents the union of the fixed and volatile principles of the alchemical work. 

Riding upon these lions are two women with flowing hair. The woman on the left holds a heart-shaped vessel from which emerges a central **Sun** flanked by two **Crescent Moons**. The woman on the right holds a similar vessel containing a **Sun** and a single **Crescent Moon**. These figures represent the celestial influences—the solar and lunar qualities—that must be balanced and multiplied.

Standing behind the woman on the right, upon the back of the lion, is a **knight** in full plate armor. He raises a sword above his head, signifying the strength and decisive action required by the adept. This knight is the Orpheus mentioned in the text, who possesses the "great riches" of the perfected Stone. In the distance, a fortified town with high towers suggests the "Philosophical City," the goal of the Great Work. 

In the bottom right corner, the engraver has signed his work: <note>original: "J. Gobille. fe.", Latin "fecit," meaning "J. Gobille made this"</note>.

<summary>This page contains the emblematic illustration for the Eleventh Key, using the imagery of fighting lions, celestial vessels, and the knight Orpheus to symbolize the alchemical process of multiplication.</summary>

# KEY XII.

<image-desc>
A detailed alchemical engraving from Basil Valentine's "Twelve Keys". In the center, a man dressed in 17th-century attire (a doublet with buttons, voluminous breeches, and a conical hat) stands in a laboratory holding a pair of long tongs. To the right, a large barrel-shaped furnace (athanor) <note>An athanor is a "slow-burning" furnace used by alchemists to maintain a constant heat over long periods.</note> with iron hoops and a small arched opening at the base emits thick, swirling clouds of smoke and bright flames. 

In the background, a workbench holds various items: apothecary jars on a high shelf, books, a brush, and a plant with two large flowers. Between the two flowers is the alchemical symbol for Mercury <note>☿</note>. A window above the bench reveals a crescent moon on the left and a radiant sun with a human face on the right.

# THE TWELVE KEYS

My imagination <note>In the 17th century, "imagination" referred to the creative and intuitive faculty of the mind</note> has persuaded me to explain more clearly, by putting into an abridged form, the book that I had previously brought to light. I have done this by the torch that I have lit, making it shine more brightly in order to better illuminate and discover our desired Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life</note> for those who are lovers of the Art <term>Art: The "Great Work" or practice of alchemy</term> and curious to know Nature. 

And although I know well that many will say that I have taught everything more than too much, and that because of this I have burdened my conscience with many sins, I will nevertheless answer them that this is still obscure enough to the ignorant and people of little wit, but clear and manifest to the children of science <note>A traditional term for initiated alchemists who possess the "true" knowledge</note>. This is why you must listen and weigh my words well; follow what they will teach you, and you will arrive directly at the most hidden mysteries of Art and Nature.

# BOOK II.

Now, you will find this summary in the true and simple instructions that follow; for I do not strive therein to use affected or deceitful words, but rather to follow the naked truth.

I have taught in the preceding treatise that all things are born and composed of three, namely: of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of fluidity and transformation</note>, of <term>Sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur represents the principle of combustion and soul</note>, and of <term>Salt</term> <note>Salt represents the principle of fixity and the physical body</note>; and this is a certain thing.

# OF THE THIRD SALT
## principle of the work of the Philosophers.

Salt, according to how it is prepared, has diverse effects, rendering the body sometimes fixed and sometimes volatile. For the spirit of Salt of Tartar <note>Potassium carbonate derived from wine lees</note>, drawn out without any other ingredient, renders all Metals volatile through resolution and putrefaction, and reduces them into a living Mercury <note>original: "Mercure vif"; referring to quicksilver or the essential metallic principle</note>, just as my *Minerals* <note>Refers to a specific treatise or section of the author's work</note> teach you. Salt of Tartar is also greatly fixed of its own nature, especially if one adds quicklime <note>Calcium oxide</note> with its heat, for being joined together they have a marvelous fixative virtue. According to how one prepares the

# BOOK II.

Vegetable Salt of Tartar <note>Potassium tartrate, derived from the sediment in wine barrels</note>, can be made both fixed and volatile, which is an admirable secret of nature and a marvelous effect of the Philosophical art <note>A reference to Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art" or "Philosophical Art"</note>.

A volatile and very clear Salt is made from the urine of a man who, for some time, has drunk nothing but pure wine. This Salt dissolves all fixed things and carries them with it through the alembic <note>A chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distillation</note>. Nevertheless, it does not remain fixed, even though this man has drunk only wine, from which this Salt of Tartar is drawn through his urine. For a certain transmutation <note>The process of changing one substance into another</note> has occurred within the human body, by which the vegetable part—that is to say, the vegetable spirit of the wine—has been changed into an animal part—that is to say, into the animal spirit of the Salt of urine. This is similar to how, for example, the transmutation of oats, hay, and other such things occurs within horses.

<vocab>Vegetable Salt of Tartar, Volatile Salt, urine, alembic, transmutation, vegetable spirit, animal spirit, Tartar</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of creating a volatile salt from the urine of a wine-drinker, illustrating the biological transmutation of vegetable spirits into animal spirits. It highlights the human body's role as a natural laboratory capable of altering the fundamental properties of substances.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Salt of Tartar, distillation, transmutation, vegetable spirit, animal spirit, urine, wine, biological chemistry</keywords>

# BOOK II.

I tell you further, that the spirit of Common Salt <note>Hydrochloric acid</note> joined with the spirit of wine <note>Ethanol/Alcohol</note>, and distilled three times with it, becomes sweet and loses all corrosion and acrimony. This spirit no longer fights physically against Gold, but if one melts it over the lime of Gold <note>Gold calx or oxide</note> duly prepared, it draws out its great redness; and if one proceeds as is necessary, the lime gives and imprints upon the purified Moon <note>Silver</note> a color similar to that which the body from which it took its origin first had.

This body can receive its first color by mixing and joining with the lascivious Venus <note>Copper</note>, insofar as it took its birth with her from the beginning from her blood, or at least from something similar to hers, and I will say no more to you about it.

This accords with what Raymond Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and logician to whom many later alchemical treatises were attributed.</note> and other Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists or "Philosophers by Fire."</note> say: that the entire <term>magistery</term> <note>The mastery or completion of the alchemical process.</note> and the work thereof is nothing but dissolving and congealing. # AZOTH,

## OR THE MEANS OF MAKING the hidden Gold of the Philosophers.

### *By Brother Basil Valentine.*

Revised, corrected & expanded by Mr. L'agneau, Physician. <note>David L'agneau was a 17th-century French physician known for his commentaries on hermetic texts.</note>

<insert>The Elder</insert> <insert>Adolphus</insert> <note>These names refer to the figures in the central illustration, representing the transmission of wisdom from an old master to a younger student.</note>

<image-desc>A symbolic alchemical woodcut. In the center is a "Philosophical Tree" growing from a small patch of earth. The tree has five main branches at the top, each ending in a many-pointed star containing a planetary/metallic symbol: Venus (Copper), Mercury (Quicksilver) at the highest point, Saturn (Lead), Mars (Iron), and Jupiter (Tin). A large equilateral triangle points upwards, enclosing the upper branches. Below, two figures stand on either side of the trunk: on the left, an older bearded man ("The Elder") in a long robe and wide-brimmed hat holding a staff; on the right, a younger man ("Adolphus") in 17th-century aristocratic dress with a sword and a feathered hat. The older man points toward a symbol of Earth or Antimony while the younger points toward a square within a downward-pointing triangle that connects the two figures. To the left of the tree is a personified, radiating Sun, and to the right is a personified crescent Moon.</image-desc>

->AT PARIS.<-

->At the shop of **Pierre Moet**, Sworn Bookseller, near the St. Michael Bridge at the Sign of St. Alexis.<- <note>original: "l'Image S. Alexis." In the 17th century, shops were identified by hanging signs rather than street numbers.</note>

->1659.<-

<summary>This is the title page for the 1659 edition of "Azoth," a famous alchemical work attributed to the legendary monk Basil Valentine and edited by the physician David L'agneau. The page features a complex symbolic woodcut illustrating the "Philosophical Tree" and the relationship between planetary metals and the celestial bodies.</summary>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece to the 1659 edition of "Azoth." The primary feature of this page is a complex alchemical woodcut known as the "Philosophical Tree." Because the book was closed while the ink was still fresh during production, a mirrored "offset" (ink transfer) of the text from the facing title page is visible here.</meta>

<image-desc>
A detailed alchemical woodcut illustration known as the "Arbor Philosophica" or Philosophical Tree. A central tree with a robust trunk grows from a small mound. Its branches spread symmetrically to the left and right. Superimposed on the upper half of the tree is a large equilateral triangle. At the very top of the central branch is a large seven-pointed star containing a human face, representing the Sun. Six smaller seven-pointed stars (representing the other planetary metals) are placed on the outer branches, three on each side. To the left of the tree, a man dressed as a pilgrim or wanderer is depicted with a wide-brimmed hat, a staff in his hand, and a pack on his back. Above the pilgrim's head is a winged or flaming disk. To the right of the tree, a shrouded or seated figure is visible in the background. The entire scene is enclosed within a thin rectangular border.
</image-desc>

# A Z O T H <note>Azoth is a central concept in alchemy, representing the universal solvent or the "Alpha and Omega" of the Great Work.</note>

I see you accusing the length of time while in such perfect health, even though you have not endured the violence of any illness, being in this flower of your age. I wish, therefore, that you would consider these things to be valued with more deliberation; for you see that I have acquired these things in less time than I have spent in the length of my life. It is not permitted to pass time in idleness, but rather carefully and with diligence to devote oneself to the knowledge of God and His works, and to employ the strength of our senses therein. For we are created in the Image of God for this purpose, and not in the likeness of beasts, which were produced for our use. Let our eyes, therefore, be op- <note>original: "ou-", catchword for "ouvert" (open)</note>

## *Adolphe.*

You have spoken so extensively of all these things, my Old Man, that I have barely retained any part of them to which I might respond. I see clearly that one must follow the good with all diligence and care, and I do not think it wise to hasten to respond to all points at once, but rather slowly, and after having reflected upon them well.

## *Le Vieillard.*

You must learn, my friend, the things that you confess to still be ignorant of; for I confess that by means of the sages and the ancients <note>The "ancients" refers to the classical and medieval alchemical authorities often cited in Hermetic literature.</note>, I know the easy and desired path. Do not despair of being able to reach it, provided that you bring to it the required will and diligence.

<meta>This page continues the moral allegory or parable regarding the proper use of wealth and the dangers of inheritance without virtue, likely serving as a metaphor for alchemical knowledge.</meta>

-># Azoth<-

I have seen with my own eyes, in a certain city, a very rich man overflowing with goods. He was the father of several children, yet he was greedy and stingy, denying even himself any comfort because of his avarice. He amassed great treasures for his children, who were raised by their mother in a total abundance of things. Assured of their father's wealth, they spent their time in idleness, luxury, and debauchery. As they grew in age, so too did their wickedness and their lifestyle multiply. When the father passed away, they spent their paternal inheritance prodigally every day on feasts and banquets. Plunged into vice and wickedness, they—fools that they were—expected their riches to continue increasing (just as had been done before).

### *Adolphe.*

I am greatly marveled to see you speak of the mysteries of the celestial doctrine <note>The "celestial doctrine" refers to divine or theological teachings, often contrasted with "earthly" philosophy.</note> and of spiritual things, because there are few people devoted to this secret <note>original: "secret" - here meaning a hidden or sacred craft, likely alchemy or hermetic philosophy</note> who are accustomed to contemplating these matters; and you have written these things so prolixly <note>at great length</note> and obscurely that everyone would more easily desire riches than the Holy Scripture. # A Z O T H

We must employ our care and diligence in such a manner that we may acquire Divine Wisdom <note>The "Sophia" or spiritual insight sought by alchemists</note>, which is, in truth, wickedly driven away by many, and is not received into the home, as *in times past she was received by Abraham, by Lot, and by the Virgin Mother of God; for in them she dwelt,* and prepared for herself in their hearts a firm and stable habitation. Now, she does not dwell in those who are superb and arrogant, and who do not seek after wisdom; for she seeks out those whom she loves.

To therefore arrive entirely at this art: Great labor or pain is not required, and the expenses are small, the instruments of little value: for this Art <note>The "Great Work" or the process of transmutation</note> can be learned in less than twelve hours, and in the space of eight days, brought to perfection, when it contains its own principle within itself, even though in other arts a course of six or seven years is required before they are rendered perfect. ## The words of Hermes to the Poimandres.

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'L' set within a square woodcut border featuring foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

The <term>Poimandres</term> <note>The name signifies the "Shepherd of Men," a divine intelligence or Mind that reveals sacred truths to Hermes.</note> of <term>Hermes Trimegistus</term> <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure combining the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note> says: As I was once reflecting upon the nature of things, and raising the subtlety of my spirit toward Heaven, having my bodily senses then lulled to sleep—as commonly happens to those who are oppressed by slumber due to excessive fullness or from weariness and vexation—the Latin says, *a certain punishment of indefinite measure* <note>original: "Quendam pæna quid menſura indefinita." This refers to the physical body acting as a heavy weight or "punishment" that binds the soul during sleep.</note>, suddenly it seemed to me that I saw a very great bodily statue, which, calling me by my name, asked me: "What do you wish to hear and see? What is it that you desire

the Poimandres <note>original: "Pimandre"; the name refers to the "Shepherd of Men," a divine intellect in Hermetic tradition</note> and desire to know, then I asked him who he was. And he said to me, "Keep a good memory, and I will teach you everything you wish to learn." As he said these things, he changed form, and all in an instant, all things were revealed to me in a moment.

## *The Emerald Tablet <note>original: "Table Smaragdine"; a legendary cryptic text said to contain the secrets of the primordial substance and its transmutations</note> of Hermes, or the words of the secrets of Hermes.*

<image-desc>A decorative square initial letter 'C' featuring ornate floral and vine motifs within the block.</image-desc>

This is true and far removed from all falsehood: that which is below is like that which is above; by this are acquired and performed the wonders of the work of a single thing. And as all things are made by one, and by the meditation <note>or contemplation</note> of one: so all things are made from one by conjunction <term>conjunction: the chemical or spiritual union of opposite elements</term>. The Sun is its father, and the Moon its Mother; the wind carries it in its belly, and the earth is its nurse, the mother of all perfection. Its power—

# SECOND PART.

## First Matter. <note>original: "Matiere Premiere." The fundamental, unformed substance that alchemists believed could be transformed into the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

I am the venomous Dragon, being present everywhere and at a low price; the thing upon which I rest, and which rests upon me, will be found within me, by whoever will search well and diligently for my water and my fire, both destroyer and composer. <note>The "Dragon" is a common alchemical metaphor for the raw, chaotic state of matter (the "Materia Prima") which must be "slain" or purified to reach a higher state.</note>

The scene is flanked by four stars containing alchemical symbols. The dragon rests on a winged globe marked with longitude and latitude lines, featuring an alchemical symbol (possibly Mercury) at its top. ## SECOND PART.

You shall extract from my body the **green and red lion** <note>The "Green Lion" often refers to vitriol or raw mineral ore, while the "Red Lion" represents the perfected, heated state of the matter.</note>, for if you do not know me exactly, you will take the five hundred <note>original: "cinq cens"; likely a symbolic measurement of heat or duration</note> of my fire; a venom issues from my nostrils, ripened too soon, which has brought harm to many. Therefore, separate with artifice the **subtle** from the **thick** <note>A reference to the Emerald Tablet: "Separate the earth from the fire, the subtle from the gross."</note>, unless you should rejoice in extreme poverty. I bestow upon you the strengths of the **males** and likewise of the **females**, and also of the **Heavens** and of the **Earth**. The mysteries of my art must be treated courageously and magnanimously if you desire that I overcome the force of the fire, in which business many have lost their time, their goods, and their labor. I am the **egg of nature** <term>Philosophical Egg: the sealed vessel or the primordial matter containing the potential for the Great Work</term> known to the wise alone, who, being pious and modest, engender from me the **prepared little world** <note>original: "petit monde"; the Microcosm or the finished Philosopher's Stone which mirrors the universe.</note>.

<summary>This page provides allegorical instructions on extracting the "lion" from the alchemical body and warns of the dangers of improper heating. It identifies the matter as the "egg of nature" which contains the dualities of heaven and earth.</summary>

# SECOND PART.

## Declaration & explanation of Adolphe.

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut alchemical emblem known as the VITRIOL diagram. The central feature is a large circle containing a seven-pointed star (heptagram) emanating from a central human face. Each point of the star is associated with a number (1-7) and a planetary symbol. Around the circumference of the circle, the English translation of the Latin motto reads: "Visit the Interior of the Earth; by Rectifying, You Will Find the Hidden Stone." <note>original: "Visita Interiora Terrae Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem." This is a famous alchemical acronym for VITRIOL.</note>

Inside the spaces between the star's points are circular medallions containing symbolic scenes:
1. A bird or phoenix.
2. A unicorn.
3. A bird of prey.
4. Two birds (a pelican feeding its young with its own blood).
5. A tree with birds.
6. A figure emerging from water.
7. A lion.

After I, Adolphe, had deliberated according to the desire of my spirit to go to Rome, so that I might more diligently search out the secrets of the arts, a certain night—being away from my lodgings, constrained by the weakness of my strength and become heavy with sleep, and greatly weakened because of the rains and storms that had occurred throughout the day—I entered into a certain cavern underground, of which the number is quite large in Rome. And having made my prayer to God, most good and most great <note>original: "Dieu tres-bon & tres-grãd," a vernacular rendering of the Latin "Deo Optimo Maximo," a common Renaissance invocation</note>, imploring his aid, being fasting and drowsy, I fell asleep. # PHILOSOPHICAL POEM ON THE AZOTH OF THE PHILOSOPHERS.

### By the Lord of NUISEMENT. <note>Clovis Hesteau, Sieur de Nuisement (c. 1550–1624), was a prominent French alchemist and poet who sought to bridge the gap between literature and the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>

If Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy or the human skill of transforming matter.</note> could create the principles of things,
Just as it can fulfill the powers enclosed
Within created principles, and multiply them;
Nature would come to humble herself at the feet of Art,
Instead of him bowing and reclining before her;
For if he possesses any glory, she is its origin.
Like an expert mistress, and he like an expert assistant.
She prepares her provisions, with which he afterwards serves her.
The immediate principles from which this great worker <note>Referring to Nature as the "great worker" or divine craftswoman.</note>
Composes the first matter <term>First Matter: The "Materia Prima," the formless primordial substance required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</term> of metals,

<summary>This introductory verse of Nuisement's poem explores the hierarchical relationship between Nature and Alchemy, arguing that the Alchemist (Art) serves as an assistant who perfects what Nature provides.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Nature vs Art, Azoth, First Matter, Clovis Hesteau de Nuisement, Principles of Matter</keywords>

--- Page 396 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic exposition of alchemical theory, specifically focusing on the subterranean generation of metals and the role of Nature as the divine artisan.</meta>

# S T A N C E S.

And those from whom the elixir <term>elixir: the transformative substance sought by alchemists to perfect matter</term> must be formed by art?
To reform the defects of imperfect bodies <note>original: "corps imparfaits" — base metals like lead or tin, believed to be "sick" or "incomplete" versions of gold</note>,
They are in being, in substance, and in virtues uniform,
Alike in qualities, but different in forms.
Nature prepares them; and in preparing them;
She renders their appearance different to our eyes.

In the center of the earth she keeps her workshop,
Where with admirable skill she assembles and fabricates
From the first principles these proximate principles;
From which she goes about forming with her expert hands
A confused mass, where by weight she assembles,
The four qualities of the two sperms <term>two sperms: the alchemical "Sulfur" and "Mercury," the masculine and feminine seeds of all metals</term> together.

Having mixed the dry water <note>original: "l'eau seiche" — a common paradox for Mercury or the universal solvent</note> with the stinking spirit,
She ignites her furnace; and goes about transmuting them
Into a smoky substance, or vapor which without ceasing
Rises, unless some opposing obstacle forces it down.

If nothing represses it by the force of its flight,
It escapes as a fugitive; and goes to form in the air
Some instrument of lightning: or the prophetic sight
Of a wandering Comet, and Meteoric fire.

But finding a rampart that it cannot pierce,
It is reverberated, and bent back downwards.
Then spreading out under pressure, into the narrowest veins
Of the frowning rocks, and haughty mountains.

There it is retained with the powerful effort
Of mineral virtue <note>original: "vertu minerall" — the inherent formative power within the earth</note>; uniting itself to it.
By the very firm bond of everlasting union,
By the sweet action of amiable heat;
Which persists day and night, in order to convert,
Into metal, the vapor which can no longer exit.

Thus then Nature has for all materials <note>original: "estoffes"</note>

# S T A N C E S .

This double vapor common to the Philosophers;
Which she renders accomplished, as much as is permitted
By both the time and the place where the vapor settles,
For if it encounters an impure matrix <note>The "matrix" or womb refers to the alchemical vessel or the base earth where the transformation occurs.</note>,
The embryo formed therein is stained by its vice;
And if the greedy hand of the eager merchant
Goes snatching the child from the maternal womb
Before the first years destined for their being;
It is an abortive fruit, which dies before it is 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;born.

The clear-sighted Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note> opened with a Lynx's eye
The earth even to its center; and subtly discovered
The deepest secrets where envious nature
Employs her industrious hand while hiding herself.

He saw her marry Mercury with Venus;
Who in the beloved bed, interlaced and naked,
Engendered the child where their sexes assemble;
Resembling both, yet resembling neither. <note>A reference to the Rebis or the Hermaphrodite, the "two-fold thing" representing the union of opposites in the Great Work.</note>

Venus, feeling herself pregnant, explored by fate
The birth and the death of her dear Embryo:
Three diverse Oracles afflicted her in her confusion,
And yet not one of them abused her with lies:
<margin>see this and what follows similar to the word sword</margin>
The first presages for her a son subject to the iron:
The other promised her a daughter for the wave:
Then the third announces to her a new brood,
Which being born daughter and son, is neither male nor female:
And whose frail life must expire in the air.
These contrary destinies make Venus sigh;
Full of impatience, awaiting the day
When the triple destiny of her fruit shall hatch.

# STANZAS.

His birth, conforming to divine omens,
Makes him believe the words of the three diviners regarding his death;
He is born male-female, and is neither man nor woman;
The sword, the wave, and the air robbed him of his soul,
Killed, drowned, and hanged, in the April of his years;
Honored by the beautiful name of his divine parents.
<note>This refers to the "Triple Death" of the alchemical subject, a common paradox where the matter must be destroyed in three ways to be purified.</note>

The blind man will take this mystery for a fable,
Which to those with unsealed eyes is a true history.
For the true principles, allied by nature,
Are those divine lovers bound by the yoke of Hymen <note>Hymen: the Greek god of marriage, symbolizing the chemical wedding of opposites.</note>:
And the double vapor which exhales from these two,
Carrying from each an equal portion,
Is this Hermaphrodite; in whom are contained
The two divine seeds of Mercury and Venus.
<note>Mercury and Venus here represent the philosophical principles of Spirit and Soul, or the metallic seeds.</note>

Art, imitating Nature, accomplishes the entire work,
By the same practice, and the same matter;
In the belly of a clear vessel, rounded like a globe,
Uniting the agent to the patient, both well-purified:
From which the fire gives birth to a subtle vapor,
Which many times rises, and many times distills;
Depriving the bodies that produce it of their life,
Then reducing itself back into them with their own soul.

This is <term>Azoth</term> <note>Azoth: the universal medicine or solvent, often symbolized by the mercury of the philosophers.</note>, it is the spirit, it is the fugitive soul,
Which, as invisible smoke, arrives circling
At the top of our globe; where losing strength and heart,
It visibly falls back as a pearly liquor:
And not at all the common quicksilver, cold and humid,
Even though it appears shining and fluid:

And one names Azoth the vapor that comes out of it.
Elixir is the body reduced into <term>Mercury</term>;
And the Azoth is the spirit which is produced from the two:
All becomes water, by water; but water that wets nothing,
And joins itself only to its own remains.

Now one can divide this <term>Great Work</term> <note>original: "grand œuvre"; the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note> into three parts,
And under three diverse names disguise the secret.
<term>Rebis</term> <note>Latin: "res bina," meaning a double-thing; the union of opposites like male and female or spirit and body</note> is the first, when the stone is composed:
And when the two joined make nothing but one thing.
Elixir the second, when in our coffin
Floats a sea of silver under veils of mourning <note>A reference to the "nigredo" or black stage of alchemy, symbolizing death and decay before rebirth</note>.

Of the transparent globe, a lucid vapor
Rises out of its waves, and condenses at the top:
Then falls back when the strength in its wings fails.
A spirit that ravishes the soul, and hides it in its breast,
When from the rotted bodies it tears away its <term>tincture</term> <note>The soul or essence of the metal that carries the power of transformation</note>.
Tincture, oil, soul, sulfur, extracted by our agent:

# S T A N C E S.

<margin>Living water under the appearance of spirit and of air</margin>
Living water that shines, and rolls as clear as silver;
A brilliant appearance, fluid and changing,
Of an air purified from this floating sea.

<margin>As after the soul and the spirit. Body and C. <note>Likely "Body and Compost" or "Body and Corpus"</note></margin>
Just as after the earth has taken back its water,
The soul and the spirit will be contained beneath the bodies;
This is the earth where the gold must die and rot,
Like the human seed in the human womb.

One sees the vegetables produced by the earth,
Reduced back to earth by <term>putrefaction</term> <note>In alchemy, the stage of "nigredo" or blackening, where matter must decompose to be reborn</note>:
Earth which surpasses the first in virtue,
By its <term>salt</term> <note>The stabilizing principle in the alchemical trinity of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note> which animates it, and which makes it more fertile.

Those who have practiced the routine of plowing,
Have taken care of the straw as well as the grain:
For the rotted straw, converted into fatness <note>Meaning rich fertilizer or compost</note>,
Rejoins the earth, and gives it life:

Whereby afterwards, its own grain, sown in such a field,
Is more abundantly produced and animated.

The metals have drawn their seed from <term>Mercury</term> <note>The fluid, feminine principle of matter; the "universal solvent"</note>:
It is their own earth and it alone has the power
To reduce them into itself by <term>putrefaction</term>,
To give to the perfect ones more perfection.

For our bodies submerged in the waves of Mercury,
And transmuted into it by their own rotting:
Are the fertile earth, and the fruitful fields,
Where our beautiful sown grains become more virtuous.

->I purchased this book for Portugal at a price that cost me 17 <term>Livre</term> <note>original: "ליטראות" (litra'ot). In this context, it likely refers to the French Livre or a similar regional pound-based currency.</note><-

->Friday, the 6th day of the month of <term>Sivan</term> <note>The ninth month of the Hebrew civil year, occurring in May or June.</note>, in the year 5546 <note>Corresponding to June 2, 1786.</note>. May it be for the honor of its Creator and His assistance therein.<-

<summary>A brief ownership inscription recording the purchase of the book for 17 Livres in June 1786, intended for use in Portugal.</summary>
